### Question 101: Confined Placental Mosaicism (CPM)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Confined Placental Mosaicism (CPM) involves a chromosomal abnormality present only in the placenta (derived from the trophoblast), while the fetus has a normal diploid karyotype. This discrepancy can arise from mitotic errors during early placental development, leading to mosaicism in the placental tissue but not the fetal lineage. This is distinct from true fetal trisomy or other chromosomal abnormalities affecting the fetus itself. (限制性胎盘镶嵌体 (CPM) 仅存在于胎盘 (源自滋养层细胞) 的染色体异常，而胎儿具有正常的二倍体核型。这种差异可能源于早期胎盘发育期间有丝分裂错误，导致胎盘组织镶嵌，但不影响胎儿谱系。这不同于真正的胎儿三体或影响胎儿本身的其它染色体异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old primigravida undergoes chorionic villus sampling (CVS) at 11 weeks gestation due to advanced maternal age. The CVS karyotype reveals 47,XY,+21 (trisomy 21) in 60% of cells analyzed, with the remaining 40% being normal 46,XY. Amniocentesis performed at 16 weeks gestation shows a normal 46,XY karyotype in all cells tested. Detailed fetal ultrasound at 20 weeks reveals no structural abnormalities consistent with Down syndrome (e.g., nuchal translucency, cardiac defects, duodenal atresia). The patient proceeds to term and delivers a healthy male infant with normal physical features and developmental milestones at 1 year of age. Karyotype analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes from the infant confirms a normal 46,XY karyotype.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the discrepancy between the CVS result and the normal fetal karyotype?

(A) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to a trisomic zygote, followed by mitotic loss of the abnormal chromosome in the fetal lineage.
(B) Postzygotic mitotic nondisjunction in the early embryo, resulting in mosaicism in both the placenta and the fetus, but with a higher proportion of abnormal cells in the placenta.
(C) Mitotic nondisjunction occurring specifically in the cytotrophoblast cells during early placental development, leading to trisomy 21 mosaicism confined to the placenta.
(D) Uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21 in the placenta, resulting in a pseudo-trisomic phenotype detected by standard karyotyping, while the fetus has normal disomy.
(E) A de novo balanced translocation involving chromosome 21 in the fetal lineage, leading to a phenotypically normal infant but a trisomic appearance in the placental cells due to the translocation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 38-year-old primigravida (advanced maternal age increases risk for chromosomal abnormalities).
- **CVS Result:** 47,XY,+21 mosaicism (60% trisomic, 40% normal) in placental tissue. This indicates an abnormality in the placental cells.
- **Amniocentesis Result:** Normal 46,XY karyotype in fetal cells (amniotic fluid reflects fetal cells). This indicates the fetus itself is chromosomally normal.
- **Fetal Ultrasound:** Normal, no features of Down syndrome. Consistent with a normal fetal karyotype.
- **Infant Outcome:** Healthy male infant with normal features and development. Karyotype confirms 46,XY. This definitively shows the fetus is normal.
- **Discrepancy:** The key finding is the difference between the abnormal CVS (placental) karyotype and the normal amniocentesis (fetal) and infant karyotypes.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining this discrepancy.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Confined Placental Mosaicism (CPM) describes a situation where a chromosomal abnormality is detected in placental tissue (e.g., CVS) but not in the fetus.
- The placenta originates from the trophoblast, which arises from the outer cells of the morula. The fetus originates from the inner cell mass.
- CPM arises from mitotic errors (nondisjunction or anaphase lag) occurring *after* the separation of the inner cell mass (fetal lineage) and the trophoblast (placental lineage).
- In this case, the CVS shows mosaicism (47,XY,+21/46,XY), indicating a mitotic error occurred in the *cytotrophoblast* lineage *after* the fetal lineage was established. The error led to trisomy 21 in some cells of the placenta.
- The fetus, derived from the inner cell mass, was unaffected by this post-zygotic mitotic error.
- Amniocentesis samples fetal cells (amniocytes) shed from the fetal skin, which are genetically identical to the fetus itself, confirming the normal 46,XY karyotype.
- The infant's karyotype is also normal, confirming the fetus was not affected.
- Option (C) accurately describes this mechanism: mitotic nondisjunction in the cytotrophoblast cells specifically leads to mosaicism confined to the placenta.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte would lead to a trisomic zygote (47,XY,+21). This abnormality would be present in *all* cells derived from the zygote, including both the placenta and the fetus. While mitotic loss *could* theoretically occur, it wouldn't explain the mosaicism seen in the CVS (60% trisomic, 40% normal) and the complete absence of trisomy in the fetus. This option describes a *fetal* trisomy, not CPM.
- (B): Postzygotic mitotic nondisjunction in the *early embryo* would affect both the placental and fetal lineages. This would result in mosaicism in *both* the placenta and the fetus, although the proportion of abnormal cells might differ. This contradicts the finding of a normal fetal karyotype (amniocentesis and infant).
- (D): Uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21 in the placenta means the placenta inherited both copies of chromosome 21 from one parent (e.g., the father). This can sometimes lead to a *pseudo-trisomic* appearance on karyotype because the extra chromosome 21 material is present, but it's not a true trisomy (it's a duplication of the parental chromosome). However, UPD in the placenta would not explain the mosaicism seen in the CVS (60%/40%) and would not explain the normal fetal karyotype. Furthermore, UPD typically involves the entire chromosome, not just a trisomy.
- (E): A balanced translocation involving chromosome 21 in the *fetal lineage* would result in a phenotypically normal infant (due to the balanced nature of the translocation) but would not explain the trisomic appearance in the placental cells. A translocation involves rearrangement of genetic material between chromosomes, not simply an extra copy. While a translocation *could* theoretically occur in the placenta, it wouldn't explain the specific finding of trisomy 21 mosaicism in the CVS and a normal diploid karyotype in the fetus.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Confined Placental Mosaicism (CPM) is a crucial concept for understanding discrepancies between prenatal diagnostic tests (like CVS) and fetal outcomes. It highlights that abnormalities detected in the placenta do not necessarily reflect the genetic status of the fetus. Key differentials include true fetal trisomy (abnormality present in both placenta and fetus), fetal mosaicism (abnormality present in both placenta and fetus, but in different proportions), and vanishing twin syndrome (loss of one twin, potentially leading to abnormal placental findings). Understanding the timing of mitotic errors (meiosis vs. post-zygotic mitosis) and the lineage specificity (placenta vs. fetus) is essential.

### Question 102: Amniocentesis & Fetal Cell Analysis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amniocentesis is a prenatal diagnostic procedure performed typically between 15-20 weeks gestation to obtain amniotic fluid containing fetal cells for genetic and biochemical analysis. The procedure involves ultrasound guidance for visualization and safe needle insertion into the amniotic sac. (羊膜穿刺是一种产前诊断程序，通常在妊娠15-20周进行，以获取含有胎儿细胞的羊水进行遗传和生化分析。该程序涉及超声引导，以实现可视化和安全地将针插入羊膜囊。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old primigravida (G1P0) presents for her routine 18-week prenatal visit. Her maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein (MSAFP) screening at 16 weeks was elevated (MoM = 3.5, normal < 2.5). She has no significant past medical history and no known family history of genetic disorders. Ultrasound examination confirms a singleton pregnancy with an estimated gestational age of 18 weeks and 3 days. The ultrasound reveals normal fetal anatomy, including the spine, brain, and abdominal wall, but shows polyhydramnios (amniotic fluid index = 28 cm, normal 5-24 cm). Given the elevated MSAFP and polyhydramnios, the physician recommends diagnostic testing. Amniocentesis is performed under ultrasound guidance. Following the procedure, a sample of amniotic fluid is sent for biochemical analysis and fetal karyotyping. Karyotyping reveals a 47,XX,+21 karyotype. Biochemical analysis of the amniotic fluid shows elevated levels of acetylcholinesterase (AChE).

Which of the following statements regarding the amniocentesis procedure and the subsequent findings in this case is MOST accurate?

(A) Amniocentesis is typically performed before 12 weeks of gestation to assess for neural tube defects, and the elevated AChE in amniotic fluid is specific for Down syndrome.
(B) The fetal cells obtained from amniotic fluid during amniocentesis are primarily derived from the fetal kidneys and liver, and the elevated MSAFP is primarily due to fetal renal dysfunction.
(C) Amniocentesis is usually performed between 15-20 weeks gestation, and the fetal cells in the amniotic fluid are shed primarily from the fetal skin, lungs, and gastrointestinal tract.
(D) The elevated MSAFP in this case is most likely due to maternal factors, and the karyotype result is irrelevant to the biochemical findings of elevated AChE.
(E) Amniocentesis is contraindicated in cases of polyhydramnios, and the fetal cells in amniotic fluid are derived exclusively from the placenta.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 38-year-old primigravida at 18 weeks gestation. Advanced maternal age is a risk factor for chromosomal abnormalities like Down syndrome.
- **Screening:** Elevated MSAFP (MoM = 3.5) at 16 weeks. Elevated MSAFP can be associated with neural tube defects, abdominal wall defects, or aneuploidy (like Trisomy 21).
- **Ultrasound:** Singleton pregnancy, 18 weeks 3 days gestation, normal fetal anatomy (rules out obvious structural defects causing elevated MSAFP), polyhydramnios (AFI = 28 cm). Polyhydramnios can be associated with fetal swallowing difficulties (e.g., esophageal atresia, duodenal atresia) or increased fetal urine production (less common). It can also be seen in aneuploidies.
- **Procedure:** Amniocentesis performed at 18 weeks under ultrasound guidance. This is the standard timing for the procedure.
- **Results:**
    - Karyotype: 47,XX,+21 (Trisomy 21, Down syndrome). This explains the elevated MSAFP and potentially the polyhydramnios.
    - Amniotic fluid AChE: Elevated. Elevated AChE in amniotic fluid is a marker for open neural tube defects (ONTDs) because the enzyme originates from fetal neural tissue. However, it can also be elevated in other conditions, including aneuploidies like Trisomy 21, although it's not specific.

- **Question:** Asks for the MOST accurate statement regarding the amniocentesis procedure and findings.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Amniocentesis Timing:** Standard timing is 15-20 weeks gestation. Before 12 weeks (CVS) or after 20 weeks is less common for diagnostic purposes. (Kaplan, FA25)
- **Amniocentesis Purpose:** To obtain amniotic fluid containing fetal cells (shed from skin, lungs, GI tract, kidneys, bladder, umbilical cord) for genetic analysis (karyotype, FISH, DNA sequencing) and biochemical analysis (e.g., AChE, alpha-fetoprotein). (Kaplan, FA25)
- **Source of Fetal Cells:** Fetal cells in amniotic fluid are shed from various fetal tissues, including skin (major source), lungs, gastrointestinal tract, kidneys, bladder, and umbilical cord. They are *not* primarily from kidneys and liver (Option B) or exclusively from the placenta (Option E). (Kaplan, FA25)
- **MSAFP:** Maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein. Elevated levels can indicate fetal neural tube defects (decreased fetal AFP production leads to increased maternal levels), abdominal wall defects, multiple gestations, incorrect gestational age dating, or aneuploidy (especially Trisomy 21). In this case, the aneuploidy (Trisomy 21) is the likely cause. Maternal factors can cause elevation, but the fetal karyotype confirms a fetal cause. (Kaplan, FA25)
- **Polyhydramnios:** Excess amniotic fluid. Can be caused by decreased fetal swallowing (e.g., esophageal/duodenal atresia, CNS abnormalities) or increased fetal urine production (rare). Aneuploidies like Trisomy 21 can sometimes be associated with polyhydramnios. (Kaplan, FA25)
- **AChE:** Acetylcholinesterase. Elevated levels in amniotic fluid are a sensitive marker for open neural tube defects (ONTDs) because the enzyme is produced by fetal neural tissue. While elevated in ONTDs, it can also be elevated in other conditions, including Trisomy 21, but it is not specific for Down syndrome (Option A). The primary source is fetal neural tissue, not just skin/lungs/GI. (Kaplan, FA25)
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+21 confirms Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). This is a common cause of elevated MSAFP and can be associated with polyhydramnios. (Kaplan, FA25)

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Amniocentesis is typically performed at 15-20 weeks, not before 12 weeks (that's CVS). While elevated AChE is a marker for ONTDs, it is not specific for Down syndrome. The timing and specificity are wrong.
- (B): Incorrect. The fetal cells in amniotic fluid come from multiple sources (skin, lungs, GI, kidneys, bladder, umbilical cord), not primarily kidneys and liver. Also, while elevated MSAFP can be associated with fetal conditions, attributing it solely to fetal renal dysfunction is incorrect, especially when Trisomy 21 is confirmed.
- (C): Correct. This statement accurately describes the typical timing (15-20 weeks) and the primary sources (skin, lungs, GI tract) of fetal cells found in amniotic fluid obtained via amniocentesis.
- (D): Incorrect. The elevated MSAFP is likely due to the confirmed Trisomy 21, not maternal factors. The karyotype result (Trisomy 21) is directly relevant to the biochemical findings (elevated MSAFP, potentially elevated AChE, although the latter is less specific).
- (E): Incorrect. Amniocentesis is *not* contraindicated by polyhydramnios; in fact, polyhydramnios can make the procedure easier by providing more fluid volume. The fetal cells originate from multiple fetal tissues, not exclusively the placenta.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amniocentesis is a standard prenatal diagnostic procedure performed between 15-20 weeks gestation using ultrasound guidance to obtain amniotic fluid containing fetal cells shed from various fetal tissues (skin, lungs, GI tract). It is used for karyotyping, DNA analysis, and biochemical assays. Elevated MSAFP can indicate various fetal conditions, including aneuploidy (like Trisomy 21) and neural tube defects. Elevated amniotic fluid AChE is a marker for open neural tube defects but is not specific. Key differentials for elevated MSAFP include neural tube defects, abdominal wall defects, multiple gestation, incorrect dating, and aneuploidy. Key differentials for polyhydramnios include fetal swallowing disorders and aneuploidy.

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Prenatal Diagnosis of Open Neural Tube Defects
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Elevated alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) in maternal serum or amniotic fluid, coupled with elevated acetylcholinesterase (AChE) activity in amniotic fluid, is diagnostic for open neural tube defects (ONTDs) like anencephaly or spina bifida. (Amniotic Fluid Alpha-Fetoprotein (AFAFP) 和乙酰胆碱酯酶 (AChE) 升高是开放性神经管缺陷 (ONTDs) 如无脑畸形或脊髓膨出畸形的诊断标志。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old primigravida undergoes routine second-trimester prenatal screening. Her maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein (MSAFP) level is significantly elevated at 3.5 MoM (Multiples of the Median). Subsequent ultrasound reveals a fetus with a large sacral meningocele, suggestive of spina bifida. To confirm the diagnosis and assess the severity of the neural tube defect, an amniocentesis is performed at 16 weeks gestation. Analysis of the amniotic fluid shows a markedly elevated AFP level (150 ng/mL, normal < 20 ng/mL) and significantly increased acetylcholinesterase (AChE) activity (1500 U/L, normal < 100 U/L). Karyotyping of fetal cells obtained from the amniocentesis is normal (46,XX). Which of the following biochemical findings definitively confirms the presence of an open neural tube defect in this fetus?

(A) Elevated maternal serum estriol levels.
(B) Decreased amniotic fluid lecithin-to-sphingomyelin (L/S) ratio.
(C) Elevated amniotic fluid alpha-fetoprotein (AFAFP) levels.
(D) Elevated amniotic fluid acetylcholinesterase (AChE) activity.
(E) Decreased amniotic fluid bilirubin levels.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Elevated MSAFP:** Suggests potential fetal abnormalities, including ONTDs, abdominal wall defects, or multiple gestations.
- **Ultrasound finding (sacral meningocele):** Strongly points towards spina bifida, an ONTD.
- **Amniocentesis:** Performed to obtain amniotic fluid for further diagnostic testing.
- **Elevated AFAFP (150 ng/mL):** AFP is synthesized by the fetal yolk sac, liver, and gastrointestinal tract. In ONTDs, AFP leaks from the fetal circulation and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) into the amniotic fluid, leading to elevated levels. This is a sensitive marker but not entirely specific.
- **Elevated AChE activity (1500 U/L):** AChE is primarily produced by fetal neural tissue. In ONTDs, exposed neural tissue directly releases AChE into the amniotic fluid. This finding is highly specific for ONTDs.
- **Normal Karyotype:** Rules out chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) or trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome), which can sometimes be associated with ONTDs.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical finding that *definitively confirms* the ONTD.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Open Neural Tube Defects (ONTDs):** Result from incomplete closure of the neural tube during early embryonic development (around 4 weeks gestation). Examples include anencephaly (failure of cephalic closure) and spina bifida (failure of caudal closure).
- **Alpha-Fetoprotein (AFP):** A major plasma protein produced by the fetal yolk sac and liver. It is normally present in fetal serum and CSF. In ONTDs, the defect in the neural tube and overlying skin/meninges allows AFP to leak directly from the fetal circulation and CSF into the amniotic fluid. Elevated AFAFP is a sensitive indicator but can also be elevated in other conditions (e.g., abdominal wall defects, multiple gestation, fetal demise).
- **Acetylcholinesterase (AChE):** An enzyme found in high concentrations in nervous tissue, particularly cholinergic neurons. It hydrolyzes acetylcholine. In ONTDs, the exposed neural tissue (spinal cord, brain tissue) directly secretes AChE into the amniotic fluid. The presence of elevated AChE activity in amniotic fluid is highly specific for ONTDs because AChE is not normally present in significant amounts in amniotic fluid unless there is leakage from exposed neural tissue. The combination of elevated AFAFP and elevated AChE activity in amniotic fluid provides definitive biochemical confirmation of an ONTD.
- **MSAFP:** Maternal serum AFP screening is performed between 15-20 weeks gestation. Elevated levels suggest increased risk for ONTDs or other fetal abnormalities.
- **L/S Ratio:** Lecithin-to-sphingomyelin ratio in amniotic fluid is used to assess fetal lung maturity, typically before delivery. A low ratio indicates immature lungs.
- **Estriol:** Maternal serum estriol levels are part of the triple screen (along with AFP and hCG) used for prenatal screening, primarily for aneuploidies like Down syndrome. Low estriol can be associated with trisomy 21.
- **Bilirubin:** Amniotic fluid bilirubin levels can be measured to assess fetal hemolytic disease.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Elevated maternal serum estriol levels are associated with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) screening, not specifically with ONTDs. In fact, estriol levels are often normal or slightly decreased in ONTDs. This option is incorrect.
- (B): Decreased amniotic fluid lecithin-to-sphingomyelin (L/S) ratio indicates fetal lung immaturity and is used to determine the optimal timing for delivery, especially in preterm infants. It is not related to the diagnosis of ONTDs. This option is incorrect.
- (C): Elevated amniotic fluid alpha-fetoprotein (AFAFP) levels are a sensitive marker for ONTDs, but they are not definitive because elevated levels can also occur in other conditions like abdominal wall defects (omphalocele, gastroschisis) or multiple gestations. The question asks for the *definitive* confirmation. This option is plausible but less specific than (D).
- (E): Decreased amniotic fluid bilirubin levels are not typically associated with ONTDs. Elevated levels might suggest fetal hemolytic disease (e.g., Rh incompatibility). This option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The combination of elevated amniotic fluid AFP and elevated amniotic fluid acetylcholinesterase (AChE) activity is the gold standard biochemical test for definitively diagnosing open neural tube defects (ONTDs) prenatally. Elevated MSAFP is a screening test, while elevated AFAFP is more specific but still requires confirmation. AChE is highly specific to neural tissue leakage. Key differentials for elevated MSAFP include ONTDs, abdominal wall defects, multiple gestation, and inaccurate gestational dating.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Fetal Anemia Management
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of fetal anemia diagnosis, management strategies including intrauterine transfusion (IUT) via percutaneous umbilical blood sampling (PUBS), and the biochemical/hematological rationale for choosing specific interventions. (考察胎儿贫血的诊断、管理策略，包括经皮脐静脉血采样(PUBS)进行宫内输血(IUT)，以及选择特定干预措施的生化/血液学原理。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old G2P1 woman at 24 weeks gestation is referred to a tertiary care center due to concerns about fetal anemia. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy child. During this pregnancy, routine screening revealed the mother to be Rh(D)-negative and the fetus Rh(D)-positive. Maternal antibody titers against the Rh(D) antigen have been steadily increasing. A recent fetal middle cerebral artery (MCA) peak systolic velocity (PSV) measurement via Doppler ultrasound showed a significantly elevated value of 1.8 m/s (normal <1.5 m/s). A subsequent PUBS procedure was performed. Analysis of the fetal blood sample revealed severe anemia (hemoglobin 4.5 g/dL, normal >12 g/dL), reticulocytosis (reticulocyte count 15%, normal <5%), and evidence of extravascular hemolysis (elevated bilirubin, decreased haptoglobin). A direct antiglobulin test (DAT) on the fetal blood was strongly positive for IgG. Given the severity of the fetal anemia and the gestational age, the most appropriate immediate management is:

(A) Administration of maternal Rho(D) immune globulin (RhoGAM) to the mother.
(B) Intrauterine transfusion (IUT) of Rh(D)-negative packed red blood cells (pRBCs) into the fetal umbilical vein via PUBS.
(C) Cesarean delivery at 28 weeks gestation.
(D) Administration of intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) to the mother.
(E) Intrauterine transfusion (IUT) of Rh(D)-positive packed red blood cells (pRBCs) into the fetal umbilical vein via PUBS.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old G2P1, Rh(D)-negative mother, Rh(D)-positive fetus at 24 weeks gestation. This sets the stage for Rh alloimmunization.
- **History:** Increasing maternal anti-Rh(D) antibody titers indicate sensitization and ongoing immune response against the fetal Rh(D) antigen.
- **Fetal Assessment:** Elevated fetal MCA PSV (1.8 m/s) is a strong indicator of fetal anemia, as the fetus compensates for low oxygen-carrying capacity by increasing cardiac output and blood flow velocity.
- **PUBS Findings:** Severe fetal anemia (Hb 4.5 g/dL), reticulocytosis (15%), signs of hemolysis (high bilirubin, low haptoglobin), and positive fetal DAT (IgG) confirm Rh-mediated hemolytic disease of the fetus and newborn (HDFN). The positive DAT confirms that maternal IgG antibodies have crossed the placenta and are coating fetal red blood cells, leading to their destruction.
- **Gestational Age:** 24 weeks is a critical period. Fetal viability is marginal, and severe anemia can lead to hydrops fetalis and fetal demise.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most appropriate immediate management* given the severe fetal anemia at 24 weeks.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Pathophysiology:** The mother is Rh(D)-negative (lacks the D antigen on her RBCs) and has been sensitized to the Rh(D) antigen, likely during a previous exposure (though the history states her first child was healthy, sensitization could have occurred via prior pregnancy, transfusion, or other means). She produces anti-Rh(D) IgG antibodies. These antibodies cross the placenta and bind to the Rh(D) antigen on the fetal RBCs (positive DAT). This antibody binding leads to extravascular hemolysis in the fetal spleen and liver, causing anemia, reticulocytosis (bone marrow response), hyperbilirubinemia (from heme breakdown), and decreased haptoglobin (binds free hemoglobin).
- **Diagnosis:** Elevated MCA PSV is a non-invasive marker for fetal anemia. PUBS provides definitive diagnosis by directly measuring fetal Hb, reticulocyte count, and performing DAT.
- **Management:** Severe fetal anemia (<7 g/dL, or Hb <10 g/dL with hydrops) at this gestational age requires intervention to prevent fetal demise or long-term sequelae.
- **Intrauterine Transfusion (IUT):** This is the standard treatment for severe fetal anemia. It involves injecting packed red blood cells directly into the fetal circulation, typically via the umbilical vein accessed through PUBS.
- **Choice of Blood:** Since the fetus is Rh(D)-positive and the anemia is caused by antibodies against the Rh(D) antigen, transfusing Rh(D)-positive blood would lead to immediate destruction of the transfused cells by the existing maternal antibodies coating the fetal RBCs. Therefore, Rh(D)-negative packed red blood cells must be used to avoid immediate transfusion reaction and provide functional red cells.
- **Timing:** At 24 weeks, the fetus is premature. While delivery might eventually be necessary, immediate management focuses on stabilizing the fetus by correcting the severe anemia. IUT allows the fetus to continue developing in utero, potentially reaching a more viable gestational age before delivery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Administration of maternal RhoGAM is used to prevent Rh sensitization in Rh(D)-negative mothers carrying an Rh(D)-positive fetus *before* sensitization occurs or shortly after potential exposure (e.g., delivery, miscarriage, amniocentesis). It works by preventing the mother's immune system from recognizing the fetal Rh(D) antigen. It is ineffective *after* the mother is already sensitized and producing antibodies, as is the case here (increasing titers, positive fetal DAT). RhoGAM does not treat existing fetal anemia.
- (C): Cesarean delivery at 28 weeks might be considered later if the fetus remains severely anemic despite IUTs or develops complications, but it is not the *immediate* management for severe anemia diagnosed at 24 weeks. IUT is the primary intervention to stabilize the fetus and allow further maturation. Delivering a 28-week infant with severe anemia carries significant risks of prematurity and complications related to the anemia itself.
- (D): IVIG administration to the mother is sometimes used in severe HDFN to try and block Fc receptors on fetal macrophages, reducing the destruction of antibody-coated RBCs. However, its efficacy is debated, and it is generally considered an adjunct therapy or used in less severe cases, not the primary treatment for severe anemia requiring immediate correction. IUT is the definitive treatment for severe anemia.
- (E): Intrauterine transfusion (IUT) is the correct *procedure*, but transfusing Rh(D)-positive blood would be disastrous. The maternal anti-Rh(D) antibodies present in the fetal circulation would immediately bind to and destroy the transfused Rh(D)-positive cells, negating the benefit of the transfusion and potentially worsening the hemolysis. Therefore, Rh(D)-negative blood must be used.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is the management of severe Rh-mediated fetal anemia. The immediate treatment is IUT using Rh(D)-negative blood. Understand the pathophysiology of HDFN, the role of maternal antibodies, the significance of fetal DAT, and the indications for IUT. Differentiate this from preventing sensitization (RhoGAM) or managing less severe cases. Key differentials include other causes of fetal anemia (e.g., parvovirus B19, alpha-thalassemia), which might require different management strategies, but the clinical picture here strongly points to Rh HDFN.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Prenatal Diagnosis of Fetal Anomalies
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chromosomal microarray analysis (CMA) is the recommended first-tier diagnostic test for fetuses with structural anomalies detected on prenatal ultrasound, as it can detect copy number variations (CNVs) including microdeletions and microduplications, which are a significant cause of such anomalies, often missed by standard karyotyping. (染色体微阵列分析 (CMA) 是产前超声发现的胎儿结构异常的一线诊断测试，因为它能检测拷贝数变异 (CNVs)，包括微缺失和微重复，这是标准核型分析常会遗漏的结构异常的重要原因。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old G2P1 woman undergoes a routine 20-week anatomy ultrasound. The scan reveals several fetal anomalies, including ventriculomegaly (enlarged lateral ventricles), a single umbilical artery, and mild cardiac defects (specifically, a small ventricular septal defect). The patient has no significant past medical history and no known family history of genetic disorders or congenital anomalies. Given these findings, the obstetrician recommends invasive prenatal testing for chromosomal abnormalities. The patient undergoes amniocentesis, and the obtained amniotic fluid is sent for cytogenetic analysis. Standard G-banded karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XX karyotype. However, due to the multiple structural anomalies observed, the clinician decides to proceed with a more sensitive diagnostic test. Chromosomal microarray analysis (CMA) is performed on the fetal DNA extracted from the amniotic fluid cells. The CMA results show a statistically significant deletion of approximately 1.5 Mb on chromosome 22q13.1, encompassing several genes including *TBX1*. This region is known to be associated with DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), although the specific breakpoint is slightly different. The patient is counseled about the implications of this finding.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying molecular mechanism responsible for the fetal anomalies detected in this case, as revealed by the CMA results?

(A) A point mutation in the *TBX1* gene leading to a non-functional protein.
(B) A balanced translocation involving chromosome 22, disrupting the *TBX1* gene.
(C) A microdeletion on chromosome 22q13.1, resulting in haploinsufficiency of genes within the deleted region, including *TBX1*.
(D) A uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 22, leading to the loss of imprinting control and overexpression of certain genes.
(E) A triplication of the *TBX1* gene, leading to overexpression and developmental abnormalities.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old pregnant woman at 20 weeks gestation.
- **Ultrasound Findings:** Multiple fetal structural anomalies: ventriculomegaly, single umbilical artery, small VSD. These are non-specific but raise suspicion for a genetic syndrome.
- **Initial Test:** Amniocentesis performed. Standard G-banded karyotyping is normal (46,XX). This rules out large chromosomal aneuploidies (like Trisomy 21, 18, 13) and balanced translocations detectable by standard resolution.
- **Second-Tier Test:** Chromosomal Microarray Analysis (CMA) performed due to persistent suspicion despite normal karyotype.
- **CMA Result:** Statistically significant deletion of ~1.5 Mb on chromosome 22q13.1, encompassing *TBX1*.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The 22q13.1 deletion region is near the commonly known 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge/Velocardiofacial syndrome), which is characterized by cardiac defects, facial anomalies, thymic hypoplasia, and developmental delay. Ventriculomegaly and single umbilical artery can also be associated features. The *TBX1* gene is a key gene in this region, crucial for craniofacial and cardiac development.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the anomalies, based on the CMA result.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **CMA Principle:** CMA (array comparative genomic hybridization) detects copy number variations (CNVs) – deletions or duplications – across the entire genome at a much higher resolution than standard karyotyping. It uses fluorescently labeled DNA probes (oligonucleotides) hybridized to the patient's DNA and a reference DNA sample. Differences in fluorescence intensity indicate gains (duplications) or losses (deletions) of specific genomic regions.
- **Finding:** The CMA detected a *deletion* of ~1.5 Mb on 22q13.1. This means that a segment of chromosome 22 is missing in the fetal cells.
- **Gene Involvement:** The deletion encompasses the *TBX1* gene. *TBX1* encodes the T-box transcription factor 1, a protein critical for the development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches, which give rise to the thymus and parathyroid glands, and also plays a role in heart and craniofacial development.
- **Mechanism:** The deletion results in the loss of one copy of the *TBX1* gene and potentially other genes within the deleted region. This leads to *haploinsufficiency* for these genes – meaning that having only one functional copy is not sufficient for normal development. Haploinsufficiency is a common mechanism for CNVs causing developmental disorders. The loss of *TBX1* function is a primary cause of the observed structural anomalies (cardiac defects, ventriculomegaly).
- **Relevance to 22q11.2 Deletion Syndrome:** While the deletion is specified as 22q13.1, it's close to the typical 22q11.2 region. The *TBX1* gene is located within the 22q11.2 region and is considered a major contributor to the phenotype of 22q11.2 deletion syndrome. The clinical features described (ventriculomegaly, VSD, single umbilical artery) are consistent with this spectrum of disorders.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** A point mutation would alter the DNA sequence of the *TBX1* gene but would not explain the large deletion detected by CMA. CMA detects changes in copy number, not single nucleotide changes. While a point mutation *could* cause similar symptoms, it's not the mechanism indicated by the CMA result.
- (B): **Incorrect.** A balanced translocation involves the exchange of segments between two non-homologous chromosomes, resulting in no net loss or gain of genetic material. While it can cause genetic disorders if it disrupts a gene at a breakpoint or leads to unbalanced gametes, a balanced translocation would typically not be detected as a deletion by CMA, and standard karyotyping would likely have revealed it (unless it was very small or complex, but the vignette states the karyotype was normal). Furthermore, the CMA specifically showed a *deletion*, not a translocation.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Uniparental disomy (UPD) occurs when an individual inherits two copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copies from the other parent. While UPD can cause disease (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes due to imprinting), it results in *two* copies of the region (or absence of the other parent's copy), not a *deletion* of a segment. CMA would detect a region of homozygosity (ROH) or copy number change, but the finding here is a specific deletion.
- (E): **Incorrect.** A triplication involves having three copies of a gene or region. This would lead to *overexpression* of the gene(s). CMA would detect a duplication, not a deletion. While gene overexpression can cause developmental problems, the CMA result clearly indicates a deletion, leading to gene loss (haploinsufficiency).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** CMA is the recommended first-tier test after a normal karyotype in cases of multiple fetal anomalies detected on ultrasound, as it detects submicroscopic deletions and duplications (CNVs) that are a common cause of congenital anomalies. The mechanism often involves haploinsufficiency of genes within the deleted region.
- **Differentials:** Understand the difference between CMA and standard karyotyping (resolution, types of abnormalities detected). Differentiate between deletion, duplication, point mutation, translocation, and UPD as molecular mechanisms. Recognize the clinical features associated with common microdeletion syndromes like 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge/VCFS).

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Preimplantation Genetic Testing (PGT)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the principles and applications of Preimplantation Genetic Testing (PGT), specifically PGT-A (aneuploidy screening) and PGT-M (monogenic/single gene defects), including the timing of biopsy, cell source, and potential limitations. (理解植入前遗传学检测 (PGT) 的原理和应用，特别是 PGT-A (染色体非整倍体筛查) 和 PGT-M (单基因缺陷)，包括活检时间、细胞来源和潜在局限性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old woman (G2P1) and her 38-year-old husband are undergoing in vitro fertilization (IVF) due to primary infertility after 3 years of trying to conceive. The wife has a history of two prior spontaneous abortions at 8 weeks gestation. The husband is a known carrier for cystic fibrosis (CFTR c.1521delC, resulting in the ΔF508 mutation). The couple desires to have a child but wants to avoid the risk of transmitting CF to their offspring and also wants to maximize the chance of a live birth, given the wife's advanced maternal age and history of recurrent pregnancy loss. Following successful fertilization and embryo culture to the blastocyst stage (day 5-6), a trophectoderm biopsy is performed on each of the 8 embryos. Genetic analysis reveals that 3 embryos are chromosomally normal (euploid), 2 embryos are affected by cystic fibrosis (homozygous for ΔF508), and 3 embryos show mosaicism for aneuploidy (e.g., 45,X/46,XX or 46,XY/47,XY,+21). The couple decides to transfer one of the euploid embryos. Which of the following statements regarding the genetic analysis performed on the trophectoderm biopsy is MOST accurate?

(A) The trophectoderm cells are the precursors to the fetal component of the placenta and are genetically identical to the inner cell mass (ICM) which forms the fetus. Therefore, the genetic results obtained from the trophectoderm accurately predict the genetic constitution of the resulting fetus.
(B) The trophectoderm biopsy is typically performed on day 3 cleavage-stage embryos (blastomere biopsy) to minimize the risk of damaging the developing embryo. Genetic analysis of blastomeres provides a more accurate assessment of the embryo's overall genetic status compared to trophectoderm biopsy.
(C) The trophectoderm cells give rise to the placenta, while the inner cell mass (ICM) gives rise to the fetus. Mosaicism detected in the trophectoderm biopsy indicates that the fetus derived from that embryo will also be mosaic for the same chromosomal abnormality.
(D) Preimplantation genetic testing for aneuploidy (PGT-A) using trophectoderm biopsy is generally considered more accurate than blastomere biopsy for detecting chromosomal abnormalities because trophectoderm cells have undergone more rounds of mitosis, potentially leading to a higher frequency of errors, but the larger cell size facilitates easier analysis.
(E) Since the husband is a carrier for CF, PGT-M was performed to identify embryos affected by cystic fibrosis. The genetic analysis of the trophectoderm cells accurately reflects the genotype of the inner cell mass, allowing for the selection of unaffected embryos for transfer.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old woman, history of recurrent pregnancy loss, husband is a CF carrier. This indicates a need for both PGT-A (due to maternal age and pregnancy loss risk) and PGT-M (due to paternal CF carrier status).
- **Procedure:** IVF, embryo culture to blastocyst stage (day 5-6), trophectoderm biopsy. This specifies the timing and cell source for PGT.
- **Genetic Results:** 3 euploid, 2 CF affected, 3 mosaic. This provides specific data about the embryos tested.
- **Decision:** Transfer of a euploid embryo. This confirms the clinical goal of selecting a healthy embryo.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST accurate statement regarding the genetic analysis of the trophectoderm biopsy. This requires understanding the developmental origin and genetic relationship between trophectoderm and ICM, the timing of biopsy, and the implications of mosaicism.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Blastocyst Development:** A blastocyst consists of the trophectoderm (outer layer) and the inner cell mass (ICM).
- **Trophectoderm Origin:** The trophectoderm gives rise to the placenta and extraembryonic tissues.
- **ICM Origin:** The ICM gives rise to the fetus.
- **PGT Timing:** Biopsy is typically performed at the cleavage stage (day 3, blastomere biopsy) or the blastocyst stage (day 5-7, trophectoderm biopsy).
- **Trophectoderm Biopsy:** Involves removing a few cells from the outer layer (trophectoderm) of the blastocyst.
- **Genetic Relationship:** Trophectoderm and ICM cells share a common origin from the initial cleavage divisions but diverge developmentally. While generally genetically concordant, epigenetic differences and mitotic errors can occur independently in each lineage, leading to mosaicism where one lineage (e.g., trophectoderm) has an abnormality while the other (ICM) does not, or vice versa.
- **Mosaicism:** The presence of mosaicism in the trophectoderm does *not* guarantee that the fetus (derived from the ICM) will also be mosaic for the same abnormality. The ICM might be euploid or mosaic with a different karyotype.
- **PGT-A Accuracy:** Trophectoderm biopsy is now preferred over blastomere biopsy for PGT-A because it is performed later, allowing for more extensive cell divisions, which can sometimes correct chromosomal errors, leading to a higher proportion of euploid embryos being selected. Also, the larger cell size of the trophectoderm makes handling and analysis easier. Blastomere biopsy (day 3) removes cells that will contribute to both the fetus and placenta, but trophectoderm biopsy specifically samples placental precursor cells.
- **PGT-M:** Identifies embryos affected by single-gene disorders like CF. The genetic analysis of the trophectoderm cells reflects the genotype of that specific cell population, which is generally representative of the ICM, allowing for the selection of unaffected embryos.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Trophectoderm gives rise to the placenta, not the fetus. The ICM gives rise to the fetus. While usually concordant, they are not genetically identical, and mosaicism in one lineage doesn't necessarily mean mosaicism in the other.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Trophectoderm biopsy is performed on blastocysts (day 5-6), not cleavage-stage embryos (day 3). Blastomere biopsy is the day 3 procedure. Trophectoderm biopsy is generally preferred for PGT-A due to higher accuracy and less embryo damage.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Trophectoderm biopsy is generally considered *more* accurate for PGT-A than blastomere biopsy. The statement incorrectly claims trophectoderm cells have undergone *more* rounds of mitosis leading to *higher* frequency of errors (this is the opposite of why blastomere biopsy was initially used, though trophectoderm biopsy is now preferred for accuracy). The larger cell size is an advantage, not a disadvantage related to error frequency.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While PGT-M is performed to identify affected embryos, the statement incorrectly implies that the trophectoderm cells' genotype *always* perfectly reflects the ICM's genotype. As explained above, mosaicism can occur, meaning the trophectoderm might be affected while the ICM is unaffected, or vice versa. The analysis reflects the trophectoderm cells themselves.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Key Takeaway:** Understand the distinct developmental origins of the trophectoderm (placenta) and the inner cell mass (fetus) from a blastocyst, and that mosaicism detected in the trophectoderm does not automatically imply mosaicism in the fetus. Trophectoderm biopsy is the standard for PGT-A today.
- **Differentials:** Distinguish PGT-A (aneuploidy screening) from PGT-M (monogenic disorders) and PGT-SR (structural rearrangements). Understand the difference between blastomere biopsy (day 3) and trophectoderm biopsy (day 5-6). Recognize the implications of mosaicism in PGT results.

---

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: PGT-A (Aneuploidy), PGT-M (Monogenic Disorders), and PGT-SR (Structural Rearrangements): Preventing Transmission of Inherited Genetic Pathologies
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) allows for the selection of embryos free from specific genetic abnormalities (aneuploidy, monogenic disorders, structural rearrangements) before implantation during in vitro fertilization (IVF), thereby preventing the transmission of inherited diseases.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old woman (G2P1) and her 40-year-old husband are undergoing IVF due to primary infertility. The wife has a known balanced reciprocal translocation t(14;21)(q13;q10), meaning she carries genetic material from chromosome 14 on chromosome 21 and vice versa, but has a normal phenotype because she has the correct total amount of genetic material. However, she is at high risk of producing gametes with unbalanced chromosome complements, such as trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), trisomy 14, or monosomy 14/21. The husband is a carrier for cystic fibrosis (CF), an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *CFTR* gene. He is heterozygous for the ΔF508 mutation. The couple desires to have a child free from both the unbalanced translocation and cystic fibrosis. They undergo IVF, and embryos are biopsied at the cleavage stage (day 3) for preimplantation genetic testing. PGT for structural rearrangements (PGT-SR) is performed on all embryos to screen for unbalanced chromosome complements resulting from the maternal translocation. PGT for monogenic disorders (PGT-M) is performed on all embryos to screen for the paternal ΔF508 *CFTR* mutation. PGT for aneuploidy (PGT-A) is also performed on all embryos to screen for common aneuploidies (e.g., trisomy 13, 18, 21, sex chromosome aneuploidies). The results show the following for five embryos:
- Embryo 1: Normal (PGT-A, PGT-M, PGT-SR)
- Embryo 2: Trisomy 21 (PGT-A), Carrier for ΔF508 *CFTR* (PGT-M), Normal (PGT-SR)
- Embryo 3: Normal (PGT-A), Carrier for ΔF508 *CFTR* (PGT-M), Unbalanced translocation (PGT-SR)
- Embryo 4: Normal (PGT-A), Normal (PGT-M), Normal (PGT-SR)
- Embryo 5: Trisomy 18 (PGT-A), Normal (PGT-M), Normal (PGT-SR)

Which embryo is most suitable for transfer, considering the couple's goals and the PGT results?

(A) Embryo 1, as it is free from aneuploidy, monogenic disorders, and structural rearrangements.
(B) Embryo 2, as it is free from structural rearrangements and monogenic disorders, despite having trisomy 21.
(C) Embryo 3, as it is free from aneuploidy and monogenic disorders, despite having an unbalanced translocation.
(D) Embryo 4, as it is free from aneuploidy, monogenic disorders, and structural rearrangements, but it is less suitable than Embryo 1 due to potential mosaicism.
(E) Embryo 5, as it is free from monogenic disorders and structural rearrangements, despite having trisomy 18.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The couple is undergoing IVF due to infertility.
- The wife has a balanced reciprocal translocation t(14;21), increasing the risk of unbalanced gametes (trisomy 21, trisomy 14, monosomy 14/21). This necessitates PGT-SR.
- The husband is a carrier for cystic fibrosis (CF, autosomal recessive, *CFTR* gene, ΔF508 mutation). This necessitates PGT-M.
- PGT-A is performed to screen for common aneuploidies.
- Embryo 1: Normal on all three PGT tests (A, M, SR).
- Embryo 2: Trisomy 21 (PGT-A), CF carrier (PGT-M), Normal (PGT-SR).
- Embryo 3: Normal (PGT-A), CF carrier (PGT-M), Unbalanced translocation (PGT-SR).
- Embryo 4: Normal on all three PGT tests (A, M, SR).
- Embryo 5: Trisomy 18 (PGT-A), Normal (PGT-M), Normal (PGT-SR).
- The question asks which embryo is most suitable for transfer, considering the couple's goals (avoiding aneuploidy, CF, and unbalanced translocation).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **PGT-A (Aneuploidy)**: Screens for abnormal chromosome numbers (e.g., trisomy 21, monosomy 13, sex chromosome aneuploidies). Trisomies are generally incompatible with life or cause severe developmental disabilities. Embryos 2 and 5 have aneuploidies (Trisomy 21 and Trisomy 18, respectively) and are therefore unsuitable for transfer based on the couple's goals.
- **PGT-M (Monogenic Disorder)**: Screens for specific single-gene disorders. In this case, it screens for the paternal ΔF508 *CFTR* mutation. Embryos 2 and 3 are carriers for CF. While the couple desires a child free from CF, transferring a carrier embryo is not strictly contraindicated if the other parent is also a carrier (which is not the case here, as the mother is not tested but the father is the carrier). However, the primary goal is to avoid *affected* offspring. Since the father is heterozygous, any child inheriting the ΔF508 mutation from him would also need to inherit it from the mother to be affected. Since the mother is not a carrier, transferring a carrier embryo (like 2 or 3) would result in a child who is a carrier, not affected. However, the question implies the goal is to avoid *any* transmission of the condition/carrier status, making embryos 2 and 3 less desirable than embryos 1 and 4.
- **PGT-SR (Structural Rearrangement)**: Screens for unbalanced chromosome complements resulting from parental structural rearrangements (e.g., translocations, inversions). Embryo 3 has an unbalanced translocation. This means it has an abnormal amount of genetic material from chromosomes 14 and 21, likely leading to developmental issues similar to those seen in unbalanced aneuploidies. Therefore, Embryo 3 is unsuitable for transfer.
- **Embryo Suitability**:
    - Embryo 1: Normal PGT-A, PGT-M, PGT-SR. Meets all criteria for a suitable embryo.
    - Embryo 2: Trisomy 21 (PGT-A), CF carrier (PGT-M), Normal PGT-SR. Unsuitable due to Trisomy 21.
    - Embryo 3: Normal PGT-A, CF carrier (PGT-M), Unbalanced translocation (PGT-SR). Unsuitable due to unbalanced translocation.
    - Embryo 4: Normal PGT-A, PGT-M, PGT-SR. Meets all criteria for a suitable embryo.
    - Embryo 5: Trisomy 18 (PGT-A), Normal PGT-M, Normal PGT-SR. Unsuitable due to Trisomy 18.
- **Comparing Embryo 1 and Embryo 4**: Both embryos are chromosomally normal (PGT-A, PGT-SR) and free from the specific monogenic disorder being tested for (PGT-M). Option (D) suggests Embryo 4 is less suitable than Embryo 1 due to potential mosaicism. While mosaicism is a concern with cleavage-stage biopsy (day 3), PGT results are typically reported as normal or abnormal based on the cells tested. If both embryos tested normal, they are considered suitable. There's no information in the vignette to suggest Embryo 4 is mosaic or less suitable than Embryo 1 based on the provided PGT results. Therefore, Embryo 1 is the most suitable embryo listed.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct**. Embryo 1 is normal for PGT-A, PGT-M, and PGT-SR, fulfilling the couple's stated goals of avoiding aneuploidy, CF, and unbalanced translocation.
- (B): **Incorrect**. Embryo 2 has Trisomy 21 (detected by PGT-A), which is a severe aneuploidy. This makes it unsuitable for transfer, despite being normal for PGT-SR and a carrier for CF (which is less critical than aneuploidy). Students might choose this if they focus only on the structural rearrangement aspect or incorrectly assume carrier status is the primary concern.
- (C): **Incorrect**. Embryo 3 has an unbalanced translocation (detected by PGT-SR). This results in an abnormal chromosome complement and is generally considered unsuitable for transfer, similar to aneuploidies. Students might choose this if they overlook the PGT-SR result or misinterpret the significance of an unbalanced translocation.
- (D): **Incorrect**. Embryo 4 is also normal for PGT-A, PGT-M, and PGT-SR, making it equally suitable as Embryo 1 based on the provided information. The statement that it is "less suitable than Embryo 1 due to potential mosaicism" is not supported by the vignette. Both embryos tested normal. While mosaicism is a potential limitation of cleavage-stage biopsy, it doesn't apply here as both embryos passed PGT. Students might be confused by the general knowledge about mosaicism in PGT but fail to apply it correctly in this specific scenario where both embryos have normal results.
- (E): **Incorrect**. Embryo 5 has Trisomy 18 (detected by PGT-A), another severe aneuploidy. This makes it unsuitable for transfer, despite being normal for PGT-M and PGT-SR. Students might choose this if they focus only on the monogenic or structural rearrangement aspects.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) allows selection of embryos free from specific genetic abnormalities. PGT-A screens for aneuploidy, PGT-M screens for monogenic disorders, and PGT-SR screens for unbalanced structural rearrangements. Embryos with aneuploidies or unbalanced structural rearrangements are generally considered unsuitable for transfer due to high risks of miscarriage or severe developmental abnormalities. Carrier status for recessive disorders (like CF) is less critical than aneuploidy or unbalanced rearrangements when selecting embryos for transfer, especially if the other parent is not a carrier.
**Differentials**: Consider the implications of different PGT results (aneuploidy, monogenic carrier status, structural rearrangement) and prioritize the most severe abnormalities (aneuploidy, unbalanced rearrangements) for exclusion. Understand the specific risks associated with each parental genetic condition

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Carrier Screening for Autosomal Recessive Conditions
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the principles and application of preconception carrier screening for common autosomal recessive disorders, including pan-ethnic screening (CFTR, SMN1, HBB) and targeted screening for specific populations (e.g., Ashkenazi Jewish population), and interpreting the results in the context of genetic counseling. (理解产前筛查的原理和应用，包括泛种族筛查（CFTR、SMN1、HBB）和针对特定人群的筛查（例如，阿什肯纳吉犹太人），以及在遗传咨询背景下解读结果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, Sarah, and her partner, David, are planning to conceive. Sarah is of Ashkenazi Jewish descent, and David is of Northern European descent. As part of their preconception planning, they undergo carrier screening for common autosomal recessive disorders. Sarah's screening panel includes tests for cystic fibrosis (CFTR), spinal muscular atrophy (SMN1), beta-thalassemia (HBB), Canavan disease (ASPA), Bloom syndrome (BLM), Fanconi anemia (FANCC), Gaucher disease (GBA), Mucolipidosis type IV (MLPA1), Niemann-Pick disease type A/B (NPC1), and Usher syndrome type 1F (USH1F). David's screening panel includes tests for CFTR, SMN1, HBB, and alpha-thalassemia (HBA1/HBA2).

Sarah's results show she is a carrier for CFTR (heterozygous for the c.1521dup3 mutation) and GBA (heterozygous for the N370S mutation). David's results show he is a carrier for SMN1 (heterozygous deletion of exon 7) and HBB (heterozygous for the sickle cell trait, HbAS).

Based on these carrier screening results, what is the risk that their child will be affected by cystic fibrosis?

(A) 0%
(B) 1/4 (25%)
(C) 1/2 (50%)
(D) 2/3
(E) 3/4 (75%)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The vignette describes a couple undergoing preconception carrier screening.
- Sarah is a carrier for CFTR (c.1521dup3 mutation) and GBA (N370S mutation).
- David is a carrier for SMN1 (exon 7 deletion) and HBB (HbAS, sickle cell trait).
- The question specifically asks for the risk of their child having cystic fibrosis (CF).
- Cystic fibrosis is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the CFTR gene.
- For a child to be affected with CF, they must inherit two mutated CFTR alleles (homozygous recessive).
- Sarah is a carrier (heterozygous) for CFTR, meaning she has one normal allele and one mutated allele (c.1521dup3).
- David does not carry any CFTR mutations according to the provided results.
- Therefore, the possible genotypes for their child are:
    - Inherit normal CFTR allele from Sarah and normal CFTR allele from David -> Normal (AA)
    - Inherit mutated CFTR allele from Sarah and normal CFTR allele from David -> Carrier (Aa)
- The probability of inheriting the mutated allele from Sarah is 1/2.
- The probability of inheriting the normal allele from David is 1.
- The probability of the child being a carrier (Aa) is (1/2) * 1 = 1/2.
- The probability of the child being unaffected and not a carrier (AA) is (1/2) * 1 = 1/2.
- The probability of the child inheriting two mutated CFTR alleles (aa) is 0, as David does not carry a CFTR mutation.
- The question asks for the risk of the child being *affected* by cystic fibrosis, which requires the homozygous recessive genotype (aa).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Cystic fibrosis (CF) is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the CFTR (Cystic Fibrosis Transmembrane conductance Regulator) gene located on chromosome 7.
- The CFTR gene encodes a protein that functions as a chloride channel, crucial for regulating the transport of chloride ions and water across epithelial cell membranes.
- Defective CFTR protein leads to thick, sticky mucus buildup in various organs, primarily the lungs, pancreas, liver, intestines, and reproductive tract.
- The most common mutation causing CF is the F508del mutation (deletion of phenylalanine at position 508), but many other mutations exist, including the c.1521dup3 mutation mentioned in the vignette, which causes a frameshift and premature stop codon.
- Inheritance pattern: Autosomal recessive. An individual must inherit two copies of the mutated CFTR allele (genotype aa) to be affected by CF.
- Carriers (heterozygotes, genotype Aa) have one normal allele (A) and one mutated allele (a). They typically do not show symptoms of CF because the single functional CFTR allele provides sufficient chloride transport.
- In this case, Sarah is a carrier (Aa) for CFTR (c.1521dup3 mutation). David's screening results do not indicate he is a carrier for any CFTR mutations (genotype AA).
- To determine the risk of their child having CF (genotype aa), we can use a Punnett square:
    - Sarah (Aa) x David (AA)
    - Offspring genotypes: AA (50%), Aa (50%)
- The probability of the child having the genotype aa (affected by CF) is 0%.
- The probability of the child being a carrier (Aa) is 50%.
- The probability of the child being unaffected and not a carrier (AA) is 50%.
- Therefore, the risk of their child being affected by cystic fibrosis is 0%.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): 0% - This is the correct answer. Since David is not a carrier for CFTR, there is no possibility for their child to inherit two mutated CFTR alleles (aa) and be affected by CF.
- (B): 1/4 (25%) - This would be the risk if both parents were carriers (Aa x Aa). The Punnett square would be: AA (1/4), Aa (1/2), aa (1/4). This is incorrect because David is not a carrier.
- (C): 1/2 (50%) - This represents the probability that their child will be a carrier for CFTR (genotype Aa), which is correct based on the Punnett square (Aa x AA -> AA 1/2, Aa 1/2), but it is not the risk of being *affected* by CF.
- (D): 2/3 - This probability does not correspond to any standard Mendelian inheritance pattern for a single gene disorder. It might arise from complex inheritance or incomplete penetrance, which are not relevant here.
- (E): 3/4 (75%) - This probability also does not correspond to standard Mendelian inheritance for CF. It is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Preconception carrier screening identifies individuals who carry one copy of a mutated gene for an autosomal recessive disorder, allowing couples to assess the risk of having an affected child. The risk of an affected child is 1/4 if both parents are carriers. If only one parent is a carrier, the risk is 0.
- Differential diagnosis: This question tests understanding of autosomal recessive inheritance and carrier screening. Key differentials include understanding the difference between being a carrier (heterozygous) and being affected (homozygous recessive), and applying Mendelian genetics to calculate risks based on parental carrier status. Other related concepts include X-linked recessive inheritance, dominant inheritance, and the specific disorders included in common carrier screening panels (CF, SMA, Thalassemia, etc.).

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Universal Newborn Screening: Dried Blood Spot (Guthrie Card) Tandem Mass Spectrometry Screening for PKU, Galactosemia, MSUD, MCAD, Hypothyroidism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the biochemical basis and screening methods for inborn errors of metabolism detectable via newborn screening, specifically focusing on the interpretation of tandem mass spectrometry results for amino acid and acylcarnitine profiles in the context of metabolic disorders. (理解新生儿筛查中生化基础和筛查方法，特别是针对氨基酸和酰基肉碱谱的串联质谱结果的解读，以及其在代谢性疾病中的应用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A healthy-appearing 3-day-old male infant is undergoing routine universal newborn screening. A dried blood spot sample (Guthrie card) is collected and analyzed using tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS). The MS/MS analysis reveals significantly elevated levels of C8 acylcarnitine (octanoylcarnitine) and moderately elevated levels of C10 acylcarnitine (decanoylcarnitine), along with normal levels of other acylcarnitines and amino acids. The infant's parents are asymptomatic and have no known family history of metabolic disorders. The infant is currently feeding well on breast milk.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying biochemical defect causing these findings?

(A) Deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase (PAH)
(B) Deficiency of galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT)
(C) Deficiency of branched-chain α-keto acid dehydrogenase complex (BCKDC)
(D) Deficiency of medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD)
(E) Deficiency of thyroid peroxidase (TPO)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 3-day-old male infant, healthy-appearing, routine newborn screening. This sets the context for detecting inborn errors of metabolism.
- **Screening Method:** Dried blood spot (Guthrie card) analyzed by tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS). This is the standard method for newborn screening for multiple metabolic disorders.
- **Key Lab Findings:** Significantly elevated C8 acylcarnitine (octanoylcarnitine) and moderately elevated C10 acylcarnitine (decanoylcarnitine). Normal levels of other acylcarnitines and amino acids are noted. This specific pattern is crucial.
- **Clinical Context:** Asymptomatic infant, no family history, feeding well. This suggests the disorder is likely newly presenting and hasn't yet caused severe symptoms, which is typical for newborn screening.
- **Question:** Asks for the MOST likely underlying biochemical defect.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Tandem Mass Spectrometry (MS/MS):** This technique is used to measure levels of amino acids and acylcarnitines in dried blood spots. Acylcarnitines are formed when fatty acids are attached to carnitine, facilitating their transport into the mitochondria for β-oxidation. Elevated acylcarnitine levels indicate a block in fatty acid oxidation.
- **C8 and C10 Acylcarnitines:** C8 (octanoylcarnitine) is derived from the β-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids (e.g., octanoic acid). C10 (decanoylcarnitine) is derived from the β-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids (e.g., decanoic acid). The accumulation of these specific acylcarnitines points to a defect in the β-oxidation pathway, specifically involving medium-chain fatty acids.
- **MCAD Deficiency:** Medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD) is a key enzyme in the β-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids. It catalyzes the dehydrogenation of medium-chain acyl-CoAs (C6-C12) to their corresponding enoyl-CoAs. Deficiency of MCAD leads to the accumulation of these medium-chain acyl-CoAs. These acyl-CoAs are then converted to their corresponding acylcarnitines (C6, C8, C10), which accumulate in the blood. C8 is typically the most elevated, followed by C6 and C10. This pattern perfectly matches the findings in the vignette.
- **Other Options:**
    - **(A) PKU (PAH deficiency):** Causes elevated phenylalanine levels. MS/MS screens for amino acids, and phenylalanine would be elevated, not acylcarnitines.
    - **(B) Galactosemia (GALT deficiency):** Causes accumulation of galactose-1-phosphate and galactitol. MS/MS screens for amino acids and acylcarnitines, not galactose metabolites.
    - **(C) MSUD (BCKDC deficiency):** Causes accumulation of branched-chain amino acids (leucine, isoleucine, valine) and their corresponding branched-chain α-keto acids. MS/MS screens for amino acids, and these specific amino acids/keto acids would be elevated, not acylcarnitines.
    - **(E) Hypothyroidism (TPO deficiency):** Causes low thyroid hormone levels (T4, T3). Newborn screening measures T4 levels, not amino acids or acylcarnitines.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase (PAH):** This enzyme converts phenylalanine to tyrosine. Deficiency causes Phenylketonuria (PKU). Newborn screening for PKU involves measuring phenylalanine levels in the blood. Elevated phenylalanine, not acylcarnitines, would be the characteristic finding. This option tests the understanding that PKU is an amino acid disorder detected by amino acid analysis, not acylcarnitine profiling.
- **(B) Deficiency of galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT):** This enzyme is involved in galactose metabolism. Deficiency causes classic galactosemia. Newborn screening for galactosemia typically involves measuring galactose-1-phosphate or galactitol levels (though less common now with MS/MS focus). Elevated galactose metabolites, not acylcarnitines, would be the characteristic finding. This option tests the understanding that galactosemia is a carbohydrate metabolism disorder.
- **(C) Deficiency of branched-chain α-keto acid dehydrogenase complex (BCKDC):** This enzyme complex is involved in the metabolism of branched-chain amino acids (leucine, isoleucine, valine). Deficiency causes Maple Syrup Urine Disease (MSUD). Newborn screening for MSUD involves measuring branched-chain amino acids (leucine, isoleucine, valine) and their corresponding α-keto acids in the blood. Elevated branched-chain amino acids/keto acids, not acylcarnitines, would be the characteristic finding. This option tests the understanding that MSUD is an amino acid disorder.
- **(E) Deficiency of thyroid peroxidase (TPO):** This enzyme is involved in thyroid hormone synthesis. Deficiency can cause congenital hypothyroidism. Newborn screening for hypothyroidism involves measuring T4 levels. Low T4 levels, not acylcarnitines, would be the characteristic finding. This option tests the understanding that hypothyroidism is an endocrine disorder screened separately from metabolic disorders detected by MS/MS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Newborn screening using tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) analyzes dried blood spots for amino acids and acylcarnitines to detect inborn errors of metabolism. Specific patterns of elevated acylcarnitines indicate defects in fatty acid oxidation pathways. Elevated C8 and C10 acylcarnitines are characteristic of medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD) deficiency. Key differentials include other fatty acid oxidation disorders (e.g., VLCAD deficiency - elevated C16, C18; SCAD deficiency - elevated C6, C8, C10) and amino acid disorders (e.g., PKU, MSUD) which affect amino acid levels, not acylcarnitines.

<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Sickle Cell Disease Screening
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Newborn screening for hemoglobinopathies, specifically Sickle Cell Disease (SCD), using Isoelectric Focusing (IEF) or High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) to detect abnormal hemoglobin variants like HbS. (新生儿筛查血红蛋白病，特别是使用等电聚焦 (IEF) 或高效液相色谱法 (HPLC) 检测异常血红蛋白变体，如 HbS 的镰状细胞病 (SCD)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn infant, born to parents of African descent, undergoes routine newborn screening. The screening test, performed using High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) on a dried blood spot, reveals the presence of two distinct hemoglobin peaks: one corresponding to normal adult hemoglobin (HbA) and another peak eluting earlier than HbA. The relative percentage of the early-eluting peak is approximately 35% of the total hemoglobin. Further analysis confirms the presence of Hemoglobin S (HbS). The infant is asymptomatic at birth and has normal vital signs and physical examination findings. The parents are counseled regarding the implications of this finding.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying genetic defect responsible for the abnormal HPLC result in this infant?

(A) A point mutation in the β-globin gene resulting in the substitution of valine for glutamic acid at position 6.
(B) A deletion of the α-globin gene cluster leading to decreased α-globin chain synthesis.
(C) A point mutation in the β-globin gene resulting in the substitution of glutamic acid for valine at position 6.
(D) A point mutation in the α-globin gene resulting in the substitution of valine for glutamic acid at position 6.
(E) A point mutation in the β-globin gene resulting in the substitution of aspartic acid for glutamic acid at position 6.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn screening:** Indicates a test performed early in life to detect potentially serious genetic or metabolic disorders.
- **Parents of African descent:** Increases the likelihood of certain genetic conditions prevalent in this population, such as Sickle Cell Disease.
- **HPLC on dried blood spot:** A common method for newborn screening for hemoglobinopathies. HPLC separates hemoglobin variants based on their charge and hydrophobicity.
- **Two distinct peaks:** HbA (normal adult hemoglobin) and an early-eluting peak. Early elution in HPLC typically indicates a more positively charged hemoglobin variant.
- **Early-eluting peak ~35%:** Significant proportion of an abnormal hemoglobin variant.
- **Confirmed presence of Hemoglobin S (HbS):** Identifies the specific abnormal hemoglobin variant.
- **Asymptomatic at birth:** Consistent with the early stage of Sickle Cell Disease before significant clinical manifestations typically appear.
- **Question asks for the underlying genetic defect:** Requires knowledge of the molecular basis of HbS.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Sickle Cell Disease (SCD) is caused by a mutation in the β-globin gene (HBB), located on chromosome 11.
- The normal β-globin protein is 146 amino acids long.
- The specific mutation responsible for HbS is a single nucleotide substitution (A to T) in the DNA sequence encoding the sixth amino acid residue.
- This substitution results in the replacement of the normal amino acid glutamic acid (hydrophilic, negatively charged at physiological pH) with valine (hydrophobic, neutral) at position 6 of the β-globin chain.
- This amino acid change (Glu6Val) alters the charge and hydrophobicity of the hemoglobin molecule.
- The altered charge causes HbS to migrate faster (elute earlier) in HPLC compared to normal HbA.
- The hydrophobic valine residue creates a "sticky patch" on the surface of the deoxygenated HbS molecule.
- Under low oxygen conditions, these sticky patches interact with complementary sites on other HbS molecules, leading to polymerization and the formation of long, rigid fibers.
- These fibers distort the red blood cell into a characteristic sickle shape, causing vaso-occlusion, hemolysis, and the clinical manifestations of SCD.
- Option (A) accurately describes this Glu6Val substitution in the β-globin gene.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Accurately describes the specific point mutation (β-globin gene, Glu6Val substitution) causing HbS.
- (B): Describes α-thalassemia, caused by deletions or mutations in the α-globin genes, leading to reduced α-globin chain production. This would not produce an HbS peak on HPLC and typically presents with microcytosis and hypochromia, not a specific abnormal Hb peak like HbS. The HPLC pattern in α-thalassemia might show a relative increase in HbA2 or HbF, or a decrease in HbA, but not HbS.
- (C): Describes the reverse substitution (Val6Glu), which would result in Hemoglobin A, the normal adult hemoglobin. This is incorrect as the infant has HbS detected.
- (D): Describes a mutation in the α-globin gene. HbS is a variant of the β-globin chain. Mutations in the α-globin gene cause α-globinopathies like α-thalassemia or Hemoglobin H disease, which have different HPLC patterns and clinical presentations.
- (E): Describes a different amino acid substitution (Glu6Asp) in the β-globin gene. This mutation causes Hemoglobin D-Punjab, another hemoglobin variant, but not HbS. Hemoglobin D-Punjab would also be detected by HPLC but would have a different elution profile than HbS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Newborn screening using HPLC or IEF is crucial for early detection of hemoglobinopathies like Sickle Cell Disease.
- Sickle Cell Disease is caused by a specific point mutation (A>T) in the β-globin gene, resulting in a Glu6Val substitution, producing HbS.
- HbS migrates differently on HPLC (earlier elution) due to the altered charge compared to HbA.
- Key differentials for abnormal hemoglobin peaks on newborn screening include HbC, HbD, HbE, and various thalassemias, each with distinct molecular defects and HPLC patterns.
